It supports\nhigh-availability applications with runtime features that min-\nimize fault-recovery time, and scheduling policies that re-\nduce the probability of correlated failures. Borg simpliﬁes\nlife for its users by offering a declarative job speciﬁcation\nlanguage, name service integration, real-time job monitor-\ning, and tools to analyze and simulate system behavior.\nWe present a summary of the Borg system architecture\nand features, important design decisions, a quantitative anal-\nysis of some of its policy decisions, and a qualitative ex-\namination of lessons learned from a decade of operational\nexperience with it.\n1.\nIntroduction\nThe cluster management system we internally call Borg ad-\nmits, schedules, starts, restarts, and monitors the full range\nof applications that Google runs. This paper explains how.\nBorg provides three main beneﬁts: it (1) hides the details\nof resource management and failure handling so its users can\nfocus on application development instead; (2) operates with\nvery high reliability and availability, and supports applica-\ntions that do the same; and (3) lets us run workloads across\ntens of thousands of machines effectively. Borg is not the\nﬁrst system to address these issues, but it’s one of the few op-\nerating at this scale, with this degree of resiliency and com-\npleteness. This paper is organized around these topics, con-\n† Work done while author was at Google.\n‡ Currently at University of Southern California.\nPermission to make digital or hard copies of part or all of this work for personal or\nclassroom use is granted without fee provided that copies are not made or distributed\nfor proﬁt or commercial advantage and that copies bear this notice and the full citation\non the ﬁrst page. Copyrights for third-party components of this work must be honored.\nFor all other uses, contact the owner/author(s).\nEuroSys’15,\nApril 21–24, 2015, Bordeaux, France.\nCopyright is held by the owner/author(s).\nACM 978-1-4503-3238-5/15/04.\nhttp://dx.doi.org/10.1145/2741948.2741964\nweb browsers\nBorgMaster\nlink shard\nUI shard\nBorgMaster\nlink shard\nUI shard\nBorgMaster\nlink shard\nUI shard\nBorgMaster\nlink shard\nUI shard\nCell\nScheduler\nborgcfg\ncommand-line \ntools\nweb browsers\nscheduler\nBorglet\nBorglet\nBorglet\nBorglet\nBorgMaster\nlink shard\nread/UI \nshard\nconfig \nfile\npersistent store \n(Paxos)\nFigure 1: The high-level architecture of Borg. Only a tiny fraction\nof the thousands of worker nodes are shown.\ncluding with a set of qualitative observations we have made\nfrom operating Borg in production for more than a decade.\n2.\nThe user perspective\nBorg’s users are Google developers and system administra-\ntors (site reliability engineers or SREs) that run Google’s\napplications and services. Users submit their work to Borg\nin the form of jobs, each of which consists of one or more\ntasks that all run the same program (binary). Each job runs\nin one Borg cell, a set of machines that are managed as a\nunit. The remainder of this section describes the main fea-\ntures exposed in the user view of Borg.\n2.1\nThe workload\nBorg cells run a heterogenous workload with two main parts.\nThe ﬁrst is long-running services that should “never” go\ndown, and handle short-lived latency-sensitive requests (a\nfew µs to a few hundred ms). Such services are used for\nend-user-facing products such as Gmail, Google Docs, and\nweb search, and for internal infrastructure services (e.g.,\nBigTable). The second is batch jobs that take from a few\nseconds to a few days to complete; these are much less sen-\nsitive to short-term performance ﬂuctuations. The workload\nmix varies across cells, which run different mixes of applica-\ntions depending on their major tenants (e.g., some cells are\nquite batch-intensive), and also varies over time: batch jobs\n\n\n=== PDF page 2 ===\ncome and go, and many end-user-facing service jobs see a\ndiurnal usage pattern. Borg is required to handle all these\ncases equally well.\nA representative Borg workload can be found in a publicly-\navailable month-long trace from May 2011 [80], which has\nbeen extensively analyzed (e.g., [68] and [1, 26, 27, 57]).\nMany application frameworks have been built on top of\nBorg over the last few years, including our internal MapRe-\nduce system [23], FlumeJava [18], Millwheel [3], and Pregel\n[59]. Most of these have a controller that submits a master\njob and one or more worker jobs; the ﬁrst two play a similar\nrole to YARN’s application manager [76]. Our distributed\nstorage systems such as GFS [34] and its successor CFS,\nBigtable [19], and Megastore [8] all run on Borg.\nFor this paper, we classify higher-priority Borg jobs as\n“production” (prod) ones, and the rest as “non-production”\n(non-prod). Most long-running server jobs are prod; most\nbatch jobs are non-prod. In a representative cell, prod jobs\nare allocated about 70% of the total CPU resources and rep-\nresent about 60% of the total CPU usage; they are allocated\nabout 55% of the total memory and represent about 85% of\nthe total memory usage. The discrepancies between alloca-\ntion and usage will prove important in §5.5.\n2.2\nClusters and cells\nThe machines in a cell belong to a single cluster, deﬁned by\nthe high-performance datacenter-scale network fabric that\nconnects them. A cluster lives inside a single datacenter\nbuilding, and a collection of buildings makes up a site.1\nA cluster usually hosts one large cell and may have a few\nsmaller-scale test or special-purpose cells. We assiduously\navoid any single point of failure.\nOur median cell size is about 10 k machines after exclud-\ning test cells; some are much larger. The machines in a cell\nare heterogeneous in many dimensions: sizes (CPU, RAM,\ndisk, network), processor type, performance, and capabili-\nties such as an external IP address or ﬂash storage. Borg iso-\nlates users from most of these differences by determining\nwhere in a cell to run tasks, allocating their resources, in-\nstalling their programs and other dependencies, monitoring\ntheir health, and restarting them if they fail.\n2.3\nJobs and tasks\nA Borg job’s properties include its name, owner, and the\nnumber of tasks it has. Jobs can have constraints to force\nits tasks to run on machines with particular attributes such as\nprocessor architecture, OS version, or an external IP address.\nConstraints can be hard or soft; the latter act like preferences\nrather than requirements. The start of a job can be deferred\nuntil a prior one ﬁnishes. A job runs in just one cell.\nEach task maps to a set of Linux processes running in\na container on a machine [62]. The vast majority of the\nBorg workload does not run inside virtual machines (VMs),\n1 There are a few exceptions for each of these relationships.\nbecause we don’t want to pay the cost of virtualization.\nAlso, the system was designed at a time when we had a\nconsiderable investment in processors with no virtualization\nsupport in hardware.\nA task has properties too, such as its resource require-\nments and the task’s index within the job. Most task proper-\nties are the same across all tasks in a job, but can be over-\nridden – e.g., to provide task-speciﬁc command-line ﬂags.\nEach resource dimension (CPU cores, RAM, disk space,\ndisk access rate, TCP ports,2 etc.) is speciﬁed independently\nat ﬁne granularity; we don’t impose ﬁxed-sized buckets or\nslots (§5.4). Borg programs are statically linked to reduce\ndependencies on their runtime environment, and structured\nas packages of binaries and data ﬁles, whose installation is\norchestrated by Borg.\nUsers operate on jobs by issuing remote procedure calls\n(RPCs) to Borg, most commonly from a command-line tool,\nother Borg jobs, or our monitoring systems (§2.6). Most job\ndescriptions are written in the declarative conﬁguration lan-\nguage BCL. This is a variant of GCL [12], which gener-\nates protobuf ﬁles [67], extended with some Borg-speciﬁc\nkeywords. GCL provides lambda functions to allow calcula-\ntions, and these are used by applications to adjust their con-\nﬁgurations to their environment; tens of thousands of BCL\nﬁles are over 1 k lines long, and we have accumulated tens\nof millions of lines of BCL. Borg job conﬁgurations have\nsimilarities to Aurora conﬁguration ﬁles [6].\nFigure 2 illustrates the states that jobs and tasks go\nthrough during their lifetime.\nsubmit + \naccept\nPending\nRunning\nDead\nupdate\nschedule\nupdate\nfinish, fail, kill, lost\nsubmit\nfail, kill, \nlost\nevict\nreject\nFigure 2: The state diagram for both jobs and tasks. Users can\ntrigger submit, kill, and update transitions.\nA user can change the properties of some or all of the\ntasks in a running job by pushing a new job conﬁguration\nto Borg, and then instructing Borg to update the tasks to\nthe new speciﬁcation. This acts as a lightweight, non-atomic\ntransaction that can easily be undone until it is closed (com-\nmitted). Updates are generally done in a rolling fashion, and\na limit can be imposed on the number of task disruptions\n2 Borg manages the available ports on a machine and allocates them to tasks.\n\n\n=== PDF page 3 ===\n(reschedules or preemptions) an update causes; any changes\nthat would cause more disruptions are skipped.\nSome task updates (e.g., pushing a new binary) will al-\nways require the task to be restarted; some (e.g., increasing\nresource requirements or changing constraints) might make\nthe task no longer ﬁt on the machine, and cause it to be\nstopped and rescheduled; and some (e.g., changing priority)\ncan always be done without restarting or moving the task.\nTasks can ask to be notiﬁed via a Unix SIGTERM sig-\nnal before they are preempted by a SIGKILL, so they have\ntime to clean up, save state, ﬁnish any currently-executing\nrequests, and decline new ones. The actual notice may be\nless if the preemptor sets a delay bound. In practice, a notice\nis delivered about 80% of the time.\n2.4\nAllocs\nA Borg alloc (short for allocation) is a reserved set of re-\nsources on a machine in which one or more tasks can be\nrun; the resources remain assigned whether or not they are\nused. Allocs can be used to set resources aside for future\ntasks, to retain resources between stopping a task and start-\ning it again, and to gather tasks from different jobs onto the\nsame machine – e.g., a web server instance and an associ-\nated logsaver task that copies the server’s URL logs from\nthe local disk to a distributed ﬁle system. The resources of\nan alloc are treated in a similar way to the resources of a ma-\nchine; multiple tasks running inside one share its resources.\nIf an alloc must be relocated to another machine, its tasks are\nrescheduled with it.\nAn alloc set is like a job: it is a group of allocs that reserve\nresources on multiple machines. Once an alloc set has been\ncreated, one or more jobs can be submitted to run in it. For\nbrevity, we will generally use “task” to refer to an alloc or a\ntop-level task (one outside an alloc) and “job” to refer to a\njob or alloc set.\n2.5\nPriority, quota, and admission control\nWhat happens when more work shows up than can be ac-\ncommodated? Our solutions for this are priority and quota.\nEvery job has a priority, a small positive integer. A high-\npriority task can obtain resources at the expense of a lower-\npriority one, even if that involves preempting (killing) the\nlatter. Borg deﬁnes non-overlapping priority bands for dif-\nferent uses, including (in decreasing-priority order): moni-\ntoring, production, batch, and best eﬀort (also known as\ntesting or free). For this paper, prod jobs are the ones in the\nmonitoring and production bands.\nAlthough a preempted task will often be rescheduled\nelsewhere in the cell, preemption cascades could occur if\na high-priority task bumped out a slightly lower-priority\none, which bumped out another slightly-lower priority task,\nand so on. To eliminate most of this, we disallow tasks in\nthe production priority band to preempt one another. Fine-\ngrained priorities are still useful in other circumstances –\ne.g., MapReduce master tasks run at a slightly higher priority\nthan the workers they control, to improve their reliability.\nPriority expresses relative importance for jobs that are\nrunning or waiting to run in a cell. Quota is used to decide\nwhich jobs to admit for scheduling. Quota is expressed as\na vector of resource quantities (CPU, RAM, disk, etc.) at a\ngiven priority, for a period of time (typically months). The\nquantities specify the maximum amount of resources that\na user’s job requests can ask for at a time (e.g., “20 TiB\nof RAM at prod priority from now until the end of July\nin cell xx”). Quota-checking is part of admission control,\nnot scheduling: jobs with insufﬁcient quota are immediately\nrejected upon submission.\nHigher-priority quota costs more than quota at lower-\npriority. Production-priority quota is limited to the actual\nresources available in the cell, so that a user who submits\na production-priority job that ﬁts in their quota can expect it\nto run, modulo fragmentation and constraints. Even though\nwe encourage users to purchase no more quota than they\nneed, many users overbuy because it insulates them against\nfuture shortages when their application’s user base grows.\nWe respond to this by over-selling quota at lower-priority\nlevels: every user has inﬁnite quota at priority zero, although\nthis is frequently hard to exercise because resources are over-\nsubscribed. A low-priority job may be admitted but remain\npending (unscheduled) due to insufﬁcient resources.\nQuota allocation is handled outside of Borg, and is inti-\nmately tied to our physical capacity planning, whose results\nare reﬂected in the price and availability of quota in differ-\nent datacenters. User jobs are admitted only if they have suf-\nﬁcient quota at the required priority. The use of quota re-\nduces the need for policies like Dominant Resource Fairness\n(DRF) [29, 35, 36, 66].\nBorg has a capability system that gives special privileges\nto some users; for example, allowing administrators to delete\nor modify any job in the cell, or allowing a user to access\nrestricted kernel features or Borg behaviors such as disabling\nresource estimation (§5.5) on their jobs.\n2.6\nNaming and monitoring\nIt’s not enough to create and place tasks: a service’s clients\nand other systems need to be able to ﬁnd them, even after\nthey are relocated to a new machine. To enable this, Borg\ncreates a stable “Borg name service” (BNS) name for each\ntask that includes the cell name, job name, and task number.\nBorg writes the task’s hostname and port into a consistent,\nhighly-available ﬁle in Chubby [14] with this name, which\nis used by our RPC system to ﬁnd the task endpoint. The\nBNS name also forms the basis of the task’s DNS name,\nso the ﬁftieth task in job jfoo owned by user ubar in cell\ncc would be reachable via 50.jfoo.ubar.cc.borg.google.com.\nBorg also writes job size and task health information into\nChubby whenever it changes, so load balancers can see\nwhere to route requests to.\n\n\n=== PDF page 4 ===\nAlmost every task run under Borg contains a built-in\nHTTP server that publishes information about the health of\nthe task and thousands of performance metrics (e.g., RPC\nlatencies). Borg monitors the health-check URL and restarts\ntasks that do not respond promptly or return an HTTP er-\nror code. Other data is tracked by monitoring tools for dash-\nboards and alerts on service level objective (SLO) violations.\nA service called Sigma provides a web-based user inter-\nface (UI) through which a user can examine the state of all\ntheir jobs, a particular cell, or drill down to individual jobs\nand tasks to examine their resource behavior, detailed logs,\nexecution history, and eventual fate. Our applications gener-\nate voluminous logs; these are automatically rotated to avoid\nrunning out of disk space, and preserved for a while after the\ntask’s exit to assist with debugging. If a job is not running\nBorg provides a “why pending?” annotation, together with\nguidance on how to modify the job’s resource requests to\nbetter ﬁt the cell. We publish guidelines for “conforming”\nresource shapes that are likely to schedule easily.\nBorg records all job submissions and task events, as well\nas detailed per-task resource usage information in Infrastore,\na scalable read-only data store with an interactive SQL-like\ninterface via Dremel [61]. This data is used for usage-based\ncharging, debugging job and system failures, and long-term\ncapacity planning. It also provided the data for the Google\ncluster workload trace [80].\nAll of these features help users to understand and debug\nthe behavior of Borg and their jobs, and help our SREs\nmanage a few tens of thousands of machines per person.\n3.\nBorg architecture\nA Borg cell consists of a set of machines, a logically central-\nized controller called the Borgmaster, and an agent process\ncalled the Borglet that runs on each machine in a cell (see\nFigure 1). All components of Borg are written in C++.\n3.1\nBorgmaster\nEach cell’s Borgmaster consists of two processes: the main\nBorgmaster process and a separate scheduler (§3.2). The\nmain Borgmaster process handles client RPCs that either\nmutate state (e.g., create job) or provide read-only access\nto data (e.g., lookup job). It also manages state machines\nfor all of the objects in the system (machines, tasks, allocs,\netc.), communicates with the Borglets, and offers a web UI\nas a backup to Sigma.\nThe Borgmaster is logically a single process but is ac-\ntually replicated ﬁve times. Each replica maintains an in-\nmemory copy of most of the state of the cell, and this state is\nalso recorded in a highly-available, distributed, Paxos-based\nstore [55] on the replicas’ local disks. A single elected mas-\nter per cell serves both as the Paxos leader and the state\nmutator, handling all operations that change the cell’s state,\nsuch as submitting a job or terminating a task on a ma-\nchine. A master is elected (using Paxos) when the cell is\nbrought up and whenever the elected master fails; it acquires\na Chubby lock so other systems can ﬁnd it. Electing a master\nand failing-over to the new one typically takes about 10 s, but\ncan take up to a minute in a big cell because some in-memory\nstate has to be reconstructed. When a replica recovers from\nan outage, it dynamically re-synchronizes its state from other\nPaxos replicas that are up-to-date.\nThe Borgmaster’s state at a point in time is called a\ncheckpoint, and takes the form of a periodic snapshot plus a\nchange log kept in the Paxos store. Checkpoints have many\nuses, including restoring a Borgmaster’s state to an arbitrary\npoint in the past (e.g., just before accepting a request that\ntriggered a software defect in Borg so it can be debugged);\nﬁxing it by hand in extremis; building a persistent log of\nevents for future queries; and ofﬂine simulations.\nA high-ﬁdelity Borgmaster simulator called Fauxmaster\ncan be used to read checkpoint ﬁles, and contains a complete\ncopy of the production Borgmaster code, with stubbed-out\ninterfaces to the Borglets. It accepts RPCs to make state ma-\nchine changes and perform operations, such as “schedule all\npending tasks”, and we use it to debug failures, by interact-\ning with it as if it were a live Borgmaster, with simulated\nBorglets replaying real interactions from the checkpoint ﬁle.\nA user can step through and observe the changes to the sys-\ntem state that actually occurred in the past. Fauxmaster is\nalso useful for capacity planning (“how many new jobs of\nthis type would ﬁt?”), as well as sanity checks before mak-\ning a change to a cell’s conﬁguration (“will this change evict\nany important jobs?”).\n3.2\nScheduling\nWhen a job is submitted, the Borgmaster records it persis-\ntently in the Paxos store and adds the job’s tasks to the pend-\ning queue. This is scanned asynchronously by the scheduler,\nwhich assigns tasks to machines if there are sufﬁcient avail-\nable resources that meet the job’s constraints. (The sched-\nuler primarily operates on tasks, not jobs.) The scan pro-\nceeds from high to low priority, modulated by a round-robin\nscheme within a priority to ensure fairness across users and\navoid head-of-line blocking behind a large job. The schedul-\ning algorithm has two parts: feasibility checking, to ﬁnd ma-\nchines on which the task could run, and scoring, which picks\none of the feasible machines.\nIn feasibility checking, the scheduler ﬁnds a set of ma-\nchines that meet the task’s constraints and also have enough\n“available” resources – which includes resources assigned\nto lower-priority tasks that can be evicted. In scoring, the\nscheduler determines the “goodness” of each feasible ma-\nchine. The score takes into account user-speciﬁed prefer-\nences, but is mostly driven by built-in criteria such as mini-\nmizing the number and priority of preempted tasks, picking\nmachines that already have a copy of the task’s packages,\nspreading tasks across power and failure domains, and pack-\ning quality including putting a mix of high and low priority\n\n\n=== PDF page 5 ===\ntasks onto a single machine to allow the high-priority ones\nto expand in a load spike.\nBorg originally used a variant of E-PVM [4] for scoring,\nwhich generates a single cost value across heterogeneous\nresources and minimizes the change in cost when placing\na task. In practice, E-PVM ends up spreading load across\nall the machines, leaving headroom for load spikes – but at\nthe expense of increased fragmentation, especially for large\ntasks that need most of the machine; we sometimes call this\n“worst ﬁt”.\nThe opposite end of the spectrum is “best ﬁt”, which tries\nto ﬁll machines as tightly as possible. This leaves some ma-\nchines empty of user jobs (they still run storage servers), so\nplacing large tasks is straightforward, but the tight packing\npenalizes any mis-estimations in resource requirements by\nusers or Borg. This hurts applications with bursty loads, and\nis particularly bad for batch jobs which specify low CPU\nneeds so they can schedule easily and try to run opportunis-\ntically in unused resources: 20% of non-prod tasks request\nless than 0.1 CPU cores.\nOur current scoring model is a hybrid one that tries to\nreduce the amount of stranded resources – ones that cannot\nbe used because another resource on the machine is fully\nallocated. It provides about 3–5% better packing efﬁciency\n(deﬁned in [78]) than best ﬁt for our workloads.\nIf the machine selected by the scoring phase doesn’t have\nenough available resources to ﬁt the new task, Borg preempts\n(kills) lower-priority tasks, from lowest to highest priority,\nuntil it does. We add the preempted tasks to the scheduler’s\npending queue, rather than migrate or hibernate them.3\nTask startup latency (the time from job submission to\na task running) is an area that has received and continues\nto receive signiﬁcant attention. It is highly variable, with\nthe median typically about 25 s. Package installation takes\nabout 80% of the total: one of the known bottlenecks is\ncontention for the local disk where packages are written to.\nTo reduce task startup time, the scheduler prefers to assign\ntasks to machines that already have the necessary packages\n(programs and data) installed: most packages are immutable\nand so can be shared and cached. (This is the only form of\ndata locality supported by the Borg scheduler.) In addition,\nBorg distributes packages to machines in parallel using tree-\nand torrent-like protocols.\nAdditionally, the scheduler uses several techniques to let\nit scale up to cells with tens of thousands of machines (§3.4).\n3.3\nBorglet\nThe Borglet is a local Borg agent that is present on every\nmachine in a cell. It starts and stops tasks; restarts them if\nthey fail; manages local resources by manipulating OS ker-\nnel settings; rolls over debug logs; and reports the state of the\nmachine to the Borgmaster and other monitoring systems.\n3 Exception: tasks that provide virtual machines for Google Compute En-\ngine users are migrated.\nThe Borgmaster polls each Borglet every few seconds to\nretrieve the machine’s current state and send it any outstand-\ning requests. This gives Borgmaster control over the rate of\ncommunication, avoids the need for an explicit ﬂow control\nmechanism, and prevents recovery storms [9].\nThe elected master is responsible for preparing messages\nto send to the Borglets and for updating the cell’s state with\ntheir responses. For performance scalability, each Borgmas-\nter replica runs a stateless link shard to handle the communi-\ncation with some of the Borglets; the partitioning is recalcu-\nlated whenever a Borgmaster election occurs. For resiliency,\nthe Borglet always reports its full state, but the link shards\naggregate and compress this information by reporting only\ndifferences to the state machines, to reduce the update load\nat the elected master.\nIf a Borglet does not respond to several poll messages its\nmachine is marked as down and any tasks it was running\nare rescheduled on other machines. If communication is\nrestored the Borgmaster tells the Borglet to kill those tasks\nthat have been rescheduled, to avoid duplicates. A Borglet\ncontinues normal operation even if it loses contact with the\nBorgmaster, so currently-running tasks and services stay up\neven if all Borgmaster replicas fail.\n3.4\nScalability\nWe are not sure where the ultimate scalability limit to Borg’s\ncentralized architecture will come from; so far, every time\nwe have approached a limit, we’ve managed to eliminate it.\nA single Borgmaster can manage many thousands of ma-\nchines in a cell, and several cells have arrival rates above\n10 000 tasks per minute. A busy Borgmaster uses 10–14\nCPU cores and up to 50 GiB RAM. We use several tech-\nniques to achieve this scale.\nEarly versions of Borgmaster had a simple, synchronous\nloop that accepted requests, scheduled tasks, and commu-\nnicated with Borglets. To handle larger cells, we split the\nscheduler into a separate process so it could operate in paral-\nlel with the other Borgmaster functions that are replicated for\nfailure tolerance. A scheduler replica operates on a cached\ncopy of the cell state. It repeatedly: retrieves state changes\nfrom the elected master (including both assigned and pend-\ning work); updates its local copy; does a scheduling pass\nto assign tasks; and informs the elected master of those as-\nsignments. The master will accept and apply these assign-\nments unless they are inappropriate (e.g., based on out of\ndate state), which will cause them to be reconsidered in the\nscheduler’s next pass. This is quite similar in spirit to the\noptimistic concurrency control used in Omega [69], and in-\ndeed we recently added the ability for Borg to use different\nschedulers for different workload types.\nTo improve response times, we added separate threads\nto talk to the Borglets and respond to read-only RPCs. For\ngreater performance, we sharded (partitioned) these func-\ntions across the ﬁve Borgmaster replicas §3.3. Together,\n\n\n=== PDF page 6 ===\nprod \nnon-prod\n0\n1\n2\n3\n4\n5\n6\n7\n8\nEvictions per task-week\nmachine shutdown\nother\nout of resources\nmachine failure\npreemption\nFigure 3: Task-eviction rates and causes for production and non-\nproduction workloads. Data from August 1st 2013.\nthese keep the 99%ile response time of the UI below 1 s\nand the 95%ile of the Borglet polling interval below 10 s.\nSeveral things make the Borg scheduler more scalable:\nScore caching: Evaluating feasibility and scoring a ma-\nchine is expensive, so Borg caches the scores until the prop-\nerties of the machine or task change – e.g., a task on the ma-\nchine terminates, an attribute is altered, or a task’s require-\nments change. Ignoring small changes in resource quantities\nreduces cache invalidations.\nEquivalence classes: Tasks in a Borg job usually have\nidentical requirements and constraints, so rather than deter-\nmining feasibility for every pending task on every machine,\nand scoring all the feasible machines, Borg only does fea-\nsibility and scoring for one task per equivalence class – a\ngroup of tasks with identical requirements.\nRelaxed randomization: It is wasteful to calculate fea-\nsibility and scores for all the machines in a large cell, so the\nscheduler examines machines in a random order until it has\nfound “enough” feasible machines to score, and then selects\nthe best within that set. This reduces the amount of scoring\nand cache invalidations needed when tasks enter and leave\nthe system, and speeds up assignment of tasks to machines.\nRelaxed randomization is somewhat akin to the batch sam-\npling of Sparrow [65] while also handling priorities, preemp-\ntions, heterogeneity and the costs of package installation.\nIn our experiments (§5), scheduling a cell’s entire work-\nload from scratch typically took a few hundred seconds, but\ndid not ﬁnish after more than 3 days when the above tech-\nniques were disabled. Normally, though, an online schedul-\ning pass over the pending queue completes in less than half\na second.\n4.\nAvailability\nFailures are the norm in large scale systems [10, 11, 22].\nFigure 3 provides a breakdown of task eviction causes in\n15 sample cells. Applications that run on Borg are expected\nto handle such events, using techniques such as replication,\nstoring persistent state in a distributed ﬁle system, and (if\nappropriate) taking occasional checkpoints. Even so, we try\nto mitigate the impact of these events. For example, Borg:\n• automatically reschedules evicted tasks, on a new ma-\nchine if necessary;\n• reduces correlated failures by spreading tasks of a job\nacross failure domains such as machines, racks, and\npower domains;\n• limits the allowed rate of task disruptions and the number\nof tasks from a job that can be simultaneously down\n65\n70\n75\n80\n85\n90\n95\n100\n0\n20\n40\n60\n80\n100\nCompacted size [%]\nPercentage of cells\nFigure 4: The effects of compaction. A CDF of the percentage of\noriginal cell size achieved after compaction, across 15 cells.\nduring maintenance activities such as OS or machine\nupgrades;\n• uses declarative desired-state representations and idem-\npotent mutating operations, so that a failed client can\nharmlessly resubmit any forgotten requests;\n• rate-limits ﬁnding new places for tasks from machines\nthat become unreachable, because it cannot distinguish\nbetween large-scale machine failure and a network parti-\ntion;\n• avoids repeating task::machine pairings that cause task or\nmachine crashes; and\n• recovers critical intermediate data written to local disk by\nrepeatedly re-running a logsaver task (§2.4), even if the\nalloc it was attached to is terminated or moved to another\nmachine. Users can set how long the system keeps trying;\na few days is common.\nA key design feature in Borg is that already-running tasks\ncontinue to run even if the Borgmaster or a task’s Borglet\ngoes down. But keeping the master up is still important\nbecause when it is down new jobs cannot be submitted\nor existing ones updated, and tasks from failed machines\ncannot be rescheduled.\nBorgmaster uses a combination of techniques that enable\nit to achieve 99.99% availability in practice: replication for\nmachine failures; admission control to avoid overload; and\ndeploying instances using simple, low-level tools to mini-\nmize external dependencies. Each cell is independent of the\nothers to minimize the chance of correlated operator errors\nand failure propagation. These goals, not scalability limita-\ntions, are the primary argument against larger cells.\n5.\nUtilization\nOne of Borg’s primary goals is to make efﬁcient use of\nGoogle’s ﬂeet of machines, which represents a signiﬁcant\nﬁnancial investment: increasing utilization by a few percent-\nage points can save millions of dollars. This section dis-\ncusses and evaluates some of the policies and techniques that\nBorg uses to do so.\n\n\n=== PDF page 7 ===\nA\nB\nC\nD\nE\n0\n50\n100\n150\n200\nCell\nPercentage of cell\nprod\nnon-prod\nbaseline\nunused\n(a) The left column for each cell shows the original size and the\ncombined workload; the right one shows the segregated case.\n-10\n0\n10\n20\n30\n40\n50\n60\n0\n20\n40\n60\n80\n100\nOverhead from segregation [%]\nPercentage of cells\n(b) CDF of additional machines that would be needed if we\nsegregated the workload of 15 representative cells.\nFigure 5: Segregating prod and non-prod work into different cells would need more machines. Both graphs show how many extra machines\nwould be needed if the prod and non-prod workloads were sent to separate cells, expressed as a percentage of the minimum number of\nmachines required to run the workload in a single cell. In this, and subsequent CDF plots, the value shown for each cell is derived from the\n90%ile of the different cell sizes our experiment trials produced; the error bars show the complete range of values from the trials.\n5.1\nEvaluation methodology\nOur jobs have placement constraints and need to handle rare\nworkload spikes, our machines are heterogenous, and we\nrun batch jobs in resources reclaimed from service jobs. So,\nto evaluate our policy choices we needed a more sophisti-\ncated metric than “average utilization”. After much exper-\nimentation we picked cell compaction: given a workload,\nwe found out how small a cell it could be ﬁtted into by\nremoving machines until the workload no longer ﬁtted, re-\npeatedly re-packing the workload from scratch to ensure that\nwe didn’t get hung up on an unlucky conﬁguration. This\nprovided clean termination conditions and facilitated auto-\nmated comparisons without the pitfalls of synthetic work-\nload generation and modeling [31]. A quantitative compari-\nson of evaluation techniques can be found in [78]: the details\nare surprisingly subtle.\nIt wasn’t possible to perform experiments on live produc-\ntion cells, but we used Fauxmaster to obtain high-ﬁdelity\nsimulation results, using data from real production cells\nand workloads, including all their constraints, actual lim-\nits, reservations, and usage data (§5.5). This data came\nfrom Borg checkpoints taken on Wednesday 2014-10-01\n14:00 PDT. (Other checkpoints produced similar results.)\nWe picked 15 Borg cells to report on by ﬁrst eliminating\nspecial-purpose, test, and small (< 5000 machines) cells,\nand then sampled the remaining population to achieve a\nroughly even spread across the range of sizes.\nTo maintain machine heterogeneity in the compacted cell\nwe randomly selected machines to remove. To maintain\nworkload heterogeneity, we kept it all, except for server and\nstorage tasks tied to a particular machine (e.g., the Borglets).\nWe changed hard constraints to soft ones for jobs larger than\nhalf the original cell size, and allowed up to 0.2% tasks to go\npending if they were very “picky” and could only be placed\non a handful of machines; extensive experiments showed\nthat this produced repeatable results with low variance. If\nFigure 6: Segregating users would need more machines. The total\nnumber of cells and the additional machines that would be needed\nif users larger than the threshold shown were given their own\nprivate cells, for 5 different cells.\nwe needed a larger cell than the original we cloned the orig-\ninal cell a few times before compaction; if we needed more\ncells, we just cloned the original.\nEach experiment was repeated 11 times for each cell with\ndifferent random-number seeds. In the graphs, we use an er-\nror bar to display the min and max of the number of ma-\nchines needed, and select the 90%ile value as the “result” –\nthe mean or median would not reﬂect what a system admin-\nistrator would do if they wanted to be reasonably sure that\nthe workload would ﬁt. We believe cell compaction provides\na fair, consistent way to compare scheduling policies, and it\ntranslates directly into a cost/beneﬁt result: better policies\nrequire fewer machines to run the same workload.\nOur experiments focused on scheduling (packing) a\nworkload from a point in time, rather than replaying a long-\nterm workload trace. This was partly to avoid the difﬁculties\nof coping with open and closed queueing models [71, 79],\npartly because traditional time-to-completion metrics don’t\napply to our environment with its long-running services,\npartly to provide clean signals for making comparisons,\n\n\n=== PDF page 8 ===\n2\n4\n6\n8\n10\n-20\n0\n20\n40\n60\n80\nSub-cells\nOverhead [%]\ncell A\ncell B\ncell C\ncell D\ncell E\n(a) Additional machines that would be needed as a function of\nthe number of smaller cells for ﬁve different original cells.\n-50\n0\n50\n100\n150\n200\n250\n0\n20\n40\n60\n80\n100\nOverhead from partitioning [%]\nPercentage of cells\n2 subcells\n5 subcells\n10 subcells\n(b) A CDF of additional machines that would be needed to\ndivide each of 15 different cells into 2, 5 or 10 cells.\nFigure 7: Subdividing cells into smaller ones would require more machines. Figure 4\nshows how much smaller our real-world cells would be if\nwe were to apply cell compaction to them. The baselines in\nthe graphs that follow use these compacted sizes.\n5.2\nCell sharing\nNearly all of our machines run both prod and non-prod tasks\nat the same time: 98% of the machines in shared Borg cells,\n83% across the entire set of machines managed by Borg. (We\nhave a few dedicated cells for special uses.)\nSince many other organizations run user-facing and batch\njobs in separate clusters, we examined what would happen if\nwe did the same. Figure 5 shows that segregating prod and\nnon-prod work would need 20–30% more machines in the\nmedian cell to run our workload. That’s because prod jobs\nusually reserve resources to handle rare workload spikes, but\ndon’t use these resources most of the time. Borg reclaims the\nunused resources (§5.5) to run much of the non-prod work,\nso we need fewer machines overall.\nMost Borg cells are shared by thousands of users. Figure\n6 shows why. For this test, we split off a user’s workload\ninto a new cell if they consumed at least 10 TiB of mem-\nory (or 100 TiB). Our existing policy looks good: even with\nthe larger threshold, we would need 2–16× as many cells,\nand 20–150% additional machines. Once again, pooling re-\nsources signiﬁcantly reduces costs.\nBut perhaps packing unrelated users and job types onto\nthe same machines results in CPU interference, and so we\nwould need more machines to compensate? To assess this,\nwe looked at how the CPI (cycles per instruction) changed\nfor tasks in different environments running on the same ma-\nchine type with the same clock speed. Under these condi-\ntions, CPI values are comparable and can be used as a proxy\nfor performance interference, since a doubling of CPI dou-\nbles the runtime of a CPU-bound program. The data was\ngathered from ∼12000 randomly selected prod tasks over\na week, counting cycles and instructions over a 5 minute in-\nterval using the hardware proﬁling infrastructure described\nin [83], and weighting samples so that every second of CPU\ntime is counted equally. The results were not clear-cut.\n(1) We found that CPI was positively correlated with\ntwo measurements over the same time interval: the overall\nCPU usage on the machine, and (largely independently) the\nnumber of tasks on the machine; adding a task to a machine\nincreases the CPI of other tasks by 0.3% (using a linear\nmodel ﬁtted to the data); increasing machine CPU usage by\n10% increases CPI by less than 2%. But even though the\ncorrelations are statistically signiﬁcant, they only explain 5%\nof the variance we saw in CPI measurements; other factors\ndominate, such as inherent differences in applications and\nspeciﬁc interference patterns [24, 83].\n(2) Comparing the CPIs we sampled from shared cells to\nones from a few dedicated cells with less diverse applica-\ntions, we saw a mean CPI of 1.58 (σ = 0.35) in shared cells\nand a mean of 1.53 (σ = 0.32) in dedicated cells – i.e., CPU\nperformance is about 3% worse in shared cells.\n(3) To address the concern that applications in different\ncells might have different workloads, or even suffer selection\nbias (maybe programs that are more sensitive to interference\nhad been moved to dedicated cells), we looked at the CPI of\nthe Borglet, which runs on all the machines in both types of\ncell. We found it had a CPI of 1.20 (σ = 0.29) in dedicated\ncells and 1.43 (σ = 0.45) in shared ones, suggesting that\nit runs 1.19× as fast in a dedicated cell as in a shared\none, although this over-weights the effect of lightly loaded\nmachines, slightly biasing the result in favor of dedicated\ncells.\n\n\n=== PDF page 9 ===\n0\n20\n40\n60\n80\n100\n 0.01\n  0.1\n    1\n   10\n  100\n 1000\nPercentage of tasks\nRequested limit [cores, GiB, GiB/core]\nprod CPU\nnon-prod CPU\nprod memory\nnon-prod memory\nmemory-to-CPU-ratio\nFigure 8: No bucket sizes ﬁt most of the tasks well. We tested\nthe effects of the latter by partitioning the workload for a cell\nacross multiple smaller cells – by ﬁrst randomly permuting\nthe jobs and then assigning them in a round-robin manner\namong the partitions. (A core is a processor hyperthread,\nnormalized for performance across machine types.) Figure 8\nshows that they take advantage of this granularity: there are\nfew obvious “sweet spots” in the amount of memory or CPU\ncores requested, and few obvious correlations between these\nresources. These distributions are quite similar to the ones\npresented in [68], except that we see slightly larger memory\nrequests at the 90%ile and above.\nOffering a set of ﬁxed-size containers or virtual machines,\nalthough common among IaaS (infrastructure-as-a-service)\nproviders [7, 33], would not be a good match to our needs.\nTo show this, we “bucketed” CPU core and memory resource\nlimits for prod jobs and allocs (§2.4) by rounding them up to\nthe next nearest power of two in each resource dimension,\nstarting at 0.5 cores for CPU and 1 GiB for RAM. Figure 9\nshows that doing so would require 30–50% more resources\nin the median case. The upper bound comes from allocating\nan entire machine to large tasks that didn’t ﬁt after quadru-\n\n\n=== PDF page 10 ===\n0\n40\n80\n120\n160\ncapacity\nlimit\nreservation\nusage\n0\n40\n80\n120\n160\nMem\n0\n10k\n20k\nWeek 1 (baseline)\nWeek 2 (aggressive)\nWeek 3 (medium)\nWeek 4 (baseline)\nOOMs\n[%]\nCPU [%]\nFigure 12: More aggressive resource estimation can reclaim more resources, with little effect on out-of-memory events (OOMs). (This is less\nthan the roughly 100% overhead reported in [37] because we\nsupported more than 4 buckets and permitted CPU and RAM\ncapacity to scale independently.)\n5.5\nResource reclamation\nA job can specify a resource limit – an upper bound on the\nresources that each task should be granted. The limit is used\nby Borg to determine if the user has enough quota to admit\nthe job, and to determine if a particular machine has enough\nfree resources to schedule the task. Just as there are users\nwho buy more quota than they need, there are users who\nrequest more resources than their tasks will use, because\nBorg will normally kill a task that tries to use more RAM\nor disk space than it requested, or throttle CPU to what it\nasked for. In addition, some tasks occasionally need to use\nall their resources (e.g., at peak times of day or while coping\nwith a denial-of-service attack), but most of the time do not.\nRather than waste allocated resources that are not cur-\nrently being consumed, we estimate how many resources a\ntask will use and reclaim the rest for work that can tolerate\nlower-quality resources, such as batch jobs. This whole pro-\ncess is called resource reclamation. The estimate is called\nthe task’s reservation, and is computed by the Borgmas-\nter every few seconds, using ﬁne-grained usage (resource-\nconsumption) information captured by the Borglet. The ini-\ntial reservation is set equal to the resource request (the limit);\nafter 300 s, to allow for startup transients, it decays slowly\ntowards the actual usage plus a safety margin. The reserva-\ntion is rapidly increased if the usage exceeds it.\nThe Borg scheduler uses limits to calculate feasibility\n(§3.2) for prod tasks,4 so they never rely on reclaimed re-\nsources and aren’t exposed to resource oversubscription; for\nnon-prod tasks, it uses the reservations of existing tasks so\nthe new tasks can be scheduled into reclaimed resources.\nA machine may run out of resources at runtime if the\nreservations (predictions) are wrong – even if all tasks use\n4 To be precise, high-priority latency-sensitive ones – see §6.2.\n0−20%\n20−40% 40−60% 60−80% 80−100%\n0%\n1%\n2%\n3%\n4%\nMachine CPU utilization\n \n \n[10ms, inf)\n[5ms, 10ms)\n[1ms, 5ms)\nFigure 13: Scheduling delays as a function of load. A plot of how\noften a runnable thread had to wait longer than 1ms to get access to\na CPU, as a function of how busy the machine was. In each pair of\nbars, latency-sensitive tasks are on the left, batch ones on the right.\nIn only a few percent of the time did a thread have to wait longer\nthan 5 ms to access a CPU (the white bars); they almost never had\nto wait longer (the darker bars). Data from a representative cell for\nthe month of December 2013; error bars show day-to-day variance.\nless than their limits. If this happens, we kill or throttle non-\nprod tasks, never prod ones.\nFigure 10 shows that many more machines would be\nrequired without resource reclamation. About 20% of the\nworkload (§6.2) runs in reclaimed resources in a median cell.\nWe can see more details in Figure 11, which shows the\nratio of reservations and usage to limits. A task that exceeds\nits memory limit will be the ﬁrst to be preempted if resources\nare needed, regardless of its priority, so it is rare for tasks\nto exceed their memory limit. On the other hand, CPU can\nreadily be throttled, so short-term spikes can push usage\nabove reservation fairly harmlessly.\nFigure 11 suggests that resource reclamation may be un-\nnecessarily conservative: there is signiﬁcant area between\nthe reservation and usage lines. To test this, we picked a live\nproduction cell and adjusted the parameters of its resource\nestimation algorithm to an aggressive setting for a week by\nreducing the safety margin, and then to an medium setting\nthat was mid-way between the baseline and aggressive set-\ntings for the next week, and then reverted to the baseline.\n\n\n=== PDF page 11 ===\nFigure 12 shows what happened. Reservations are clearly\ncloser to usage in the second week, and somewhat less so in\nthe third, with the biggest gaps shown in the baseline weeks\n(1st and 4th). As anticipated, the rate of out-of-memory\n(OOM) events increased slightly in weeks 2 and 3.5 After\nreviewing these results, we decided that the net gains out-\nweighed the downsides, and deployed the medium resource\nreclamation parameters to other cells.\n6.\nIsolation\n50% of our machines run 9 or more tasks; a 90%ile machine\nhas about 25 tasks and will be running about 4500 threads\n[83]. Although sharing machines between applications in-\ncreases utilization, it also requires good mechanisms to pre-\nvent tasks from interfering with one another. This applies to\nboth security and performance.\n6.1\nSecurity isolation\nWe use a Linux chroot jail as the primary security isolation\nmechanism between multiple tasks on the same machine. To\nallow remote debugging, we used to distribute (and rescind)\nssh keys automatically to give a user access to a machine\nonly while it was running tasks for the user. For most users,\nthis has been replaced by the borgssh command, which col-\nlaborates with the Borglet to construct an ssh connection to\na shell that runs in the same chroot and cgroup as the task,\nlocking down access even more tightly.\nVMs and security sandboxing techniques are used to run\nexternal software by Google’s AppEngine (GAE) [38] and\nGoogle Compute Engine (GCE). We run each hosted VM in\na KVM process [54] that runs as a Borg task.\n6.2\nPerformance isolation\nEarly versions of Borglet had relatively primitive resource\nisolation enforcement: post-hoc usage checking of memory,\ndisk space and CPU cycles, combined with termination of\ntasks that used too much memory or disk and aggressive ap-\nplication of Linux’s CPU priorities to rein in tasks that used\ntoo much CPU. But it was still too easy for rogue tasks to af-\nfect the performance of other tasks on the machine, so some\nusers inﬂated their resource requests to reduce the number of\ntasks that Borg could co-schedule with theirs, thus decreas-\ning utilization. Resource reclamation could claw back some\nof the surplus, but not all, because of the safety margins in-\nvolved. In the most extreme cases, users petitioned to use\ndedicated machines or cells.\nNow, all Borg tasks run inside a Linux cgroup-based re-\nsource container [17, 58, 62] and the Borglet manipulates the\ncontainer settings, giving much improved control because\nthe OS kernel is in the loop. Even so, occasional low-level\nresource interference (e.g., memory bandwidth or L3 cache\npollution) still happens, as in [60, 83].\n5 The anomaly at the end of week 3 is unrelated to this experiment.\nTo help with overload and overcommitment, Borg tasks\nhave an application class or appclass. The most important\ndistinction is between the latency-sensitive (LS) appclasses\nand the rest, which we call batch in this paper. LS tasks are\nused for user-facing applications and shared infrastructure\nservices that require fast response to requests. High-priority\nLS tasks receive the best treatment, and are capable of tem-\nporarily starving batch tasks for several seconds at a time.\nA second split is between compressible resources (e.g.,\nCPU cycles, disk I/O bandwidth) that are rate-based and\ncan be reclaimed from a task by decreasing its quality of\nservice without killing it; and non-compressible resources\n(e.g., memory, disk space) which generally cannot be re-\nclaimed without killing the task. If a machine runs out of\nnon-compressible resources, the Borglet immediately termi-\nnates tasks, from lowest to highest priority, until the remain-\ning reservations can be met. If the machine runs out of com-\npressible resources, the Borglet throttles usage (favoring LS\ntasks) so that short load spikes can be handled without killing\nany tasks. If things do not improve, Borgmaster will remove\none or more tasks from the machine.\nA user-space control loop in the Borglet assigns mem-\nory to containers based on predicted future usage (for prod\ntasks) or on memory pressure (for non-prod ones); handles\nOut-of-Memory (OOM) events from the kernel; and kills\ntasks when they try to allocate beyond their memory lim-\nits, or when an over-committed machine actually runs out\nof memory. Linux’s eager ﬁle-caching signiﬁcantly compli-\ncates the implementation because of the need for accurate\nmemory-accounting.\nTo improve performance isolation, LS tasks can reserve\nentire physical CPU cores, which stops other LS tasks from\nusing them. Batch tasks are permitted to run on any core,\nbut they are given tiny scheduler shares relative to the LS\ntasks. The Borglet dynamically adjusts the resource caps of\ngreedy LS tasks in order to ensure that they do not starve\nbatch tasks for multiple minutes, selectively applying CFS\nbandwidth control when needed [75]; shares are insufﬁcient\nbecause we have multiple priority levels.\nLike Leverich [56], we found that the standard Linux\nCPU scheduler (CFS) required substantial tuning to support\nboth low latency and high utilization. To reduce schedul-\ning delays, our version of CFS uses extended per-cgroup\nload history [16], allows preemption of batch tasks by LS\ntasks, and reduces the scheduling quantum when multiple LS\ntasks are runnable on a CPU. Fortunately, many of our ap-\nplications use a thread-per-request model, which mitigates\nthe effects of persistent load imbalances. We sparingly use\ncpusets to allocate CPU cores to applications with particu-\nlarly tight latency requirements. Some results of these efforts\nare shown in Figure 13. Work continues in this area, adding\nthread placement and CPU management that is NUMA-,\nhyperthreading-, and power-aware (e.g., [81]), and improv-\ning the control ﬁdelity of the Borglet.\n\n\n=== PDF page 12 ===\nTasks are permitted to consume resources up to their\nlimit. Most of them are allowed to go beyond that for com-\npressible resources like CPU, to take advantage of unused\n(slack) resources. Only 5% of LS tasks disable this, presum-\nably to get better predictability; fewer than 1% of batch tasks\ndo. Using slack memory is disabled by default, because it in-\ncreases the chance of a task being killed, but even so, 10%\nof LS tasks override this, and 79% of batch tasks do so be-\ncause it’s a default setting of the MapReduce framework.\nThis complements the results for reclaimed resources (§5.5).\nBatch tasks are willing to exploit unused as well as reclaimed\nmemory opportunistically: most of the time this works, al-\nthough the occasional batch task is sacriﬁced when an LS\ntask needs resources in a hurry.\n7.\nRelated work\nResource scheduling has been studied for decades, in con-\ntexts as varied as wide-area HPC supercomputing Grids, net-\nworks of workstations, and large-scale server clusters. We\nfocus here on only the most relevant work in the context of\nlarge-scale server clusters.\nSeveral recent studies have analyzed cluster traces from\nYahoo!, Google, and Facebook [20, 52, 63, 68, 70, 80, 82],\nand illustrate the challenges of scale and heterogeneity in-\nherent in these modern datacenters and workloads. [69] con-\ntains a taxonomy of cluster manager architectures.\nApache Mesos [45] splits the resource management and\nplacement functions between a central resource manager\n(somewhat like Borgmaster minus its scheduler) and mul-\ntiple “frameworks” such as Hadoop [41] and Spark [73]\nusing an offer-based mechanism. Borg mostly centralizes\nthese functions using a request-based mechanism that scales\nquite well. DRF [29, 35, 36, 66] was initially developed for\nMesos; Borg uses priorities and admission quotas instead.\nThe Mesos developers have announced ambitions to extend\nMesos to include speculative resource assignment and recla-\nmation, and to ﬁx some of the issues identiﬁed in [69].\nYARN [76] is a Hadoop-centric cluster manager. Each ap-\nplication has a manager that negotiates for the resources it\nneeds with a central resource manager; this is much the same\nscheme that Google MapReduce jobs have used to obtain\nresources from Borg since about 2008. YARN’s resource\nmanager only recently became fault tolerant. A related open-\nsource effort is the Hadoop Capacity Scheduler [42] which\nprovides multi-tenant support with capacity guarantees, hi-\nerarchical queues, elastic sharing and fairness. YARN has re-\ncently been extended to support multiple resource types, pri-\norities, preemptions, and advanced admission control [21].\nThe Tetris research prototype [40] supports makespan-aware\njob packing.\nFacebook’s Tupperware [64], is a Borg-like system for\nscheduling cgroup containers on a cluster; only a few details\nhave been disclosed, although it seems to provide a form\nof resource reclamation. Twitter has open-sourced Aurora\n[5], a Borg-like scheduler for long running services that runs\non top of Mesos, with a conﬁguration language and state\nmachine similar to Borg’s.\nThe Autopilot system from Microsoft [48] provides “au-\ntomating software provisioning and deployment; system\nmonitoring; and carrying out repair actions to deal with\nfaulty software and hardware” for Microsoft clusters. The\nBorg ecosystem provides similar features, but space pre-\ncludes a discussion here; Isaard [48] outlines many best\npractices that we adhere to as well.\nQuincy [49] uses a network ﬂow model to provide fairness-\nand data locality-aware scheduling for data-processing DAGs\non clusters of a few hundred nodes. Borg uses quota and pri-\norities to share resources among users and scales to tens of\nthousands of machines. Quincy handles execution graphs\ndirectly while this is built separately on top of Borg.\nCosmos [44] focuses on batch processing, with an em-\nphasis on ensuring that its users get fair access to resources\nthey have donated to the cluster. It uses a per-job manager to\nacquire resources; few details are publicly available.\nMicrosoft’s Apollo system [13] uses per-job schedulers\nfor short-lived batch jobs to achieve high throughput on clus-\nters that seem to be comparably-sized to Borg cells. Apollo\nuses opportunistic execution of lower-priority background\nwork to boost utilization to high levels at the cost of (some-\ntimes) multi-day queueing delays. Apollo nodes provide a\nprediction matrix of starting times for tasks as a function\nof size over two resource dimensions, which the schedulers\ncombine with estimates of startup costs and remote-data-\naccess to make placement decisions, modulated by random\ndelays to reduce collisions. Borg uses a central scheduler for\nplacement decisions based on state about prior allocations,\ncan handle more resource dimensions, and focuses on the\nneeds of high-availability, long-running applications; Apollo\ncan probably handle a higher task arrival rate.\nAlibaba’s Fuxi [84] supports data-analysis workloads;\nit has been running since 2009. Like Borgmaster, a cen-\ntral FuxiMaster (replicated for failure-tolerance) gathers\nresource-availability information from nodes, accepts re-\nquests from applications, and matches one to the other. The\nFuxi incremental scheduling policy is the inverse of Borg’s\nequivalence classes: instead of matching each task to one\nof a suitable set of machines, Fuxi matches newly-available\nresources against a backlog of pending work. Like Mesos,\nFuxi allows “virtual resource” types to be deﬁned. Only syn-\nthetic workload results are publicly available.\nOmega [69] supports multiple parallel, specialized “verti-\ncals” that are each roughly equivalent to a Borgmaster minus\nits persistent store and link shards. Omega schedulers use\noptimistic concurrency control to manipulate a shared repre-\nsentation of desired and observed cell state stored in a cen-\ntral persistent store, which is synced to/from the Borglets by\na separate link component. The Omega architecture was de-\nsigned to support multiple distinct workloads that have their\n\n\n=== PDF page 13 ===\nown application-speciﬁc RPC interface, state machines, and\nscheduling policies (e.g., long-running servers, batch jobs\nfrom various frameworks, infrastructure services like clus-\nter storage systems, virtual machines from the Google Cloud\nPlatform). On the other hand, Borg offers a “one size ﬁts all”\nRPC interface, state machine semantics, and scheduler pol-\nicy, which have grown in size and complexity over time as a\nresult of needing to support many disparate workloads, and\nscalability has not yet been a problem (§3.4).\nGoogle’s open-source Kubernetes system [53] places\napplications in Docker containers [28] onto multiple host\nnodes. It runs both on bare metal (like Borg) and on various\ncloud hosting providers, such as Google Compute Engine.\nIt is under active development by many of the same engi-\nneers who built Borg. Google offers a hosted version called\nGoogle Container Engine [39]. We discuss how lessons from\nBorg are being applied to Kubernetes in the next section.\nThe high-performance computing community has a long\ntradition of work in this area (e.g., Maui, Moab, Platform\nLSF [2, 47, 50]); however the requirements of scale, work-\nloads and fault tolerance are different from those of Google’s\ncells. In general, such systems achieve high utilization by\nhaving large backlogs (queues) of pending work.\nVirtualization providers such as VMware [77] and data-\ncenter solution providers such as HP and IBM [46] provide\ncluster management solutions that typically scale to O(1000)\nmachines. In addition, several research groups have proto-\ntyped systems that improve the quality of scheduling deci-\nsions in certain ways (e.g., [25, 40, 72, 74]).\nAnd ﬁnally, as we have indicated, another important part\nof managing large scale clusters is automation and “operator\nscaleout”. [43] describes how planning for failures, multi-\ntenancy, health checking, admission control, and restartabil-\nity are necessary to achieve high numbers of machines per\noperator. Borg’s design philosophy is similar and allows us\nto support tens of thousands of machines per operator (SRE).\n8.\nLessons and future work\nIn this section we recount some of the qualitative lessons\nwe’ve learned from operating Borg in production for more\nthan a decade, and describe how these observations have\nbeen leveraged in designing Kubernetes [53].\n8.1\nLessons learned: the bad\nWe begin with some features of Borg that serve as cautionary\ntales, and informed alternative designs in Kubernetes.\nJobs are restrictive as the only grouping mechanism\nfor tasks. Borg has no ﬁrst-class way to manage an entire\nmulti-job service as a single entity, or to refer to related\ninstances of a service (e.g., canary and production tracks).\nAs a hack, users encode their service topology in the job\nname and build higher-level management tools to parse these\nnames. At the other end of the spectrum, it’s not possible to\nrefer to arbitrary subsets of a job, which leads to problems\nlike inﬂexible semantics for rolling updates and job resizing.\nTo avoid such difﬁculties, Kubernetes rejects the job no-\ntion and instead organizes its scheduling units (pods) using\nlabels – arbitrary key/value pairs that users can attach to any\nobject in the system. The equivalent of a Borg job can be\nachieved by attaching a job:jobname label to a set of pods,\nbut any other useful grouping can be represented too, such\nas the service, tier, or release-type (e.g., production, stag-\ning, test). Operations in Kubernetes identify their targets by\nmeans of a label query that selects the objects that the op-\neration should apply to. This approach gives more ﬂexibility\nthan the single ﬁxed grouping of a job.\nOne IP address per machine complicates things. In\nBorg, all tasks on a machine use the single IP address of\ntheir host, and thus share the host’s port space. This causes\na number of difﬁculties: Borg must schedule ports as a re-\nsource; tasks must pre-declare how many ports they need,\nand be willing to be told which ones to use when they start;\nthe Borglet must enforce port isolation; and the naming and\nRPC systems must handle ports as well as IP addresses.\nThanks to the advent of Linux namespaces, VMs, IPv6,\nand software-deﬁned networking, Kubernetes can take a\nmore user-friendly approach that eliminates these complica-\ntions: every pod and service gets its own IP address, allowing\ndevelopers to choose ports rather than requiring their soft-\nware to adapt to the ones chosen by the infrastructure, and\nremoves the infrastructure complexity of managing ports.\nOptimizing for power users at the expense of casual\nones. Borg provides a large set of features aimed at “power\nusers” so they can ﬁne-tune the way their programs are run\n(the BCL speciﬁcation lists about 230 parameters): the ini-\ntial focus was supporting the largest resource consumers at\nGoogle, for whom efﬁciency gains were paramount. Unfor-\ntunately the richness of this API makes things harder for the\n“casual” user, and constrains its evolution. Our solution has\nbeen to build automation tools and services that run on top of\nBorg, and determine appropriate settings from experimenta-\ntion. These beneﬁt from the freedom to experiment afforded\nby failure-tolerant applications: if the automation makes a\nmistake it is a nuisance, not a disaster.\n8.2\nLessons learned: the good\nOn the other hand, a number of Borg’s design features have\nbeen remarkably beneﬁcial and have stood the test of time.\nAllocs are useful. The Borg alloc abstraction spawned\nthe widely-used logsaver pattern (§2.4) and another popular\none in which a simple data-loader task periodically updates\nthe data used by a web server. Allocs and packages allow\nsuch helper services to be developed by separate teams. The\nKubernetes equivalent of an alloc is the pod, which is a\nresource envelope for one or more containers that are always\nscheduled onto the same machine and can share resources.\nKubernetes uses helper containers in the same pod instead\nof tasks in an alloc, but the idea is the same.\n\n\n=== PDF page 14 ===\nCluster management is more than task management.\nAlthough Borg’s primary role is to manage the lifecycles\nof tasks and machines, the applications that run on Borg\nbeneﬁt from many other cluster services, including naming\nand load balancing. Kubernetes supports naming and load\nbalancing using the service abstraction: a service has a name\nand a dynamic set of pods deﬁned by a label selector. Any\ncontainer in the cluster can connect to the service using the\nservice name. Under the covers, Kubernetes automatically\nload-balances connections to the service among the pods that\nmatch the label selector, and keeps track of where the pods\nare running as they get rescheduled over time due to failures.\nIntrospection is vital. Although Borg almost always\n“just works,” when something goes wrong, ﬁnding the root\ncause can be challenging. An important design decision in\nBorg was to surface debugging information to all users rather\nthan hiding it: Borg has thousands of users, so “self-help”\nhas to be the ﬁrst step in debugging. Although this makes it\nharder for us to deprecate features and change internal poli-\ncies that users come to rely on, it is still a win, and we’ve\nfound no realistic alternative. To handle the enormous vol-\nume of data, we provide several levels of UI and debugging\ntools, so users can quickly identify anomalous events related\nto their jobs, and then drill down to detailed event and error\nlogs from their applications and the infrastructure itself.\nKubernetes aims to replicate many of Borg’s introspec-\ntion techniques. For example, it ships with tools such as\ncAdvisor [15] for resource monitoring, and log aggregation\nbased on Elasticsearch/Kibana [30] and Fluentd [32]. The\nmaster can be queried for a snapshot of its objects’ state.\nKubernetes has a uniﬁed mechanism that all components can\nuse to record events (e.g., a pod being scheduled, a container\nfailing) that are made available to clients.\nThe master is the kernel of a distributed system.\nBorgmaster was originally designed as a monolithic sys-\ntem, but over time, it became more of a kernel sitting at the\nheart of an ecosystem of services that cooperate to man-\nage user jobs. For example, we split off the scheduler and\nthe primary UI (Sigma) into separate processes, and added\nservices for admission control, vertical and horizontal auto-\nscaling, re-packing tasks, periodic job submission (cron),\nworkﬂow management, and archiving system actions for\noff-line querying. Together, these have allowed us to scale\nup the workload and feature set without sacriﬁcing perfor-\nmance or maintainability.\nThe Kubernetes architecture goes further: it has an API\nserver at its core that is responsible only for processing re-\nquests and manipulating the underlying state objects. The\ncluster management logic is built as small, composable\nmicro-services that are clients of this API server, such as\nthe replication controller, which maintains the desired num-\nber of replicas of a pod in the face of failures, and the node\ncontroller, which manages the machine lifecycle.\n8.3\nConclusion\nVirtually all of Google’s cluster workloads have switched to\nuse Borg over the past decade. We continue to evolve it, and\nhave applied the lessons we learned from it to Kubernetes.\nAcknowledgments\nThe authors of this paper performed the evaluations and\nwrote the paper, but the dozens of engineers who de-\nsigned, implemented, and maintained Borg’s components\nand ecosystem are the key to its success. We list here just\nthose who participated most directly in the design, imple-\nmentation, and operation of the Borgmaster and Borglets.\nOur apologies if we missed anybody.\nThe initial Borgmaster was primarily designed and im-\nplemented by Jeremy Dion and Mark Vandevoorde, with\nBen Smith, Ken Ashcraft, Maricia Scott, Ming-Yee Iu, and\nMonika Henzinger.
